Correlations in Uniform Spanning Trees: a Fermionic Approach
Authors: Alan Rapoport
Published: 2025

ABSTRACT
In the present paper we establish a clear correspondence between probabilities of certain edges belonging to a realization of the uniform spanning tree (UST), and the states of a fermionic Gaussian free field. Namely, we express the probabilities of given edges belonging or not to the UST in terms of fermionic Gaussian expectations. This allows us to explicitly calculate joint probability mass functions of the degree of the UST on a general finite graph, as well as obtain their scaling limits for certain regular lattices.

Introduction

In Cipriani et al. [6] the authors study the joint moments of the so-called height-one field of the Abelian sandpile model (ASM), by means of a construction of a local field with fermionic variables on a graph. This was achieved given the fact that the height-one field of the ASM at stationarity can be put into correspondence with certain realizations of the uniform spanning tree (UST) [7, 8, 14]. By doing so, the authors also managed to obtain closed-form expressions of the joint moments of the degree field of the UST. In the present paper we build up on those techniques to obtain, among other results, a closed-form expression for the probability mass function of the UST.

Our first observation is a general recipe to calculate probabilities of given edges to be or not to be in the UST in terms of fermionic variables, which is the result given in Proposition 3.6 in Section 3. so-called fermionic Gaussian free field state (fGFF) (roughly, expected values under the fGFF measure). Precise definitions will be given in Section 2, but for the moment we stress that these variables satisfy anti-commuting relations as\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ \psi (v_i)\psi (v_j) = -\psi (v_j)\psi (v_i),\quad \forall \, i,\,j, $$\end{document}ψ(vi)ψ(vj)=-ψ(vj)ψ(vi),∀i,j,and the fGFF measure is a Gaussian measure on these variables.

On the other hand, there is a well-known connection between these UST probabilities and determinants of the transfer-current matrix M, which was originally studied to model electric networks. In this context, if \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal G$$\end{document}G is considered as a network where each edge represents a conductance equal to 1, for any two edges e and f the value of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$M(e,\,f)$$\end{document}M(e,f) is the current measured through f when a battery imposes a unit current through e. These values can also be related to local times of a random walk on \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal G$$\end{document}G, so M can also be expressed in terms of gradients of the Green’s function of the graph in question (see e.g. Kassel and Wu [9]). With this ingredient, the aforementioned fermionic expected values can be written in terms of determinants of M.

At this point we should highlight that the connection between Grassmann variables and uniform spanning trees (USTs) is fundamentally explained by the Matrix Tree Theorem (MTT) (see for example Bollobás [4]), which expresses the UST partition function as the determinant of a principal minor of the graph Laplacian. On the one hand, determinants arise naturally in the computation of Grassmannian Gaussian integrals, whereas the MTT provides the link between these determinants and the UST model. From another perspective, the nilpotency of Grassmann variables truncates any power expansion to first order, making them ideal for encoding high-temperature expansions in statistical mechanics. Although in the present paper we do not use Grassmann variables to compute new observables directly if not by means of the determinantal structure known by the MTT, they serve as a representation of observables of the UST in terms of a particular fermionic field. We leverage on the simplicity of formula (1.1) to encode observables of the UST in terms of straight-forward fermionic combinations. In Liu et al. [12], the authors look to find a conformal field theory (CFT) lying behind the UST. We hope that an enhancement of our techniques may serve to determine algebras and the defining such a CFT.

We highlight that fermionic variables have already been used to study problems of random trees. See for example Caracciolo et al. [5] and Bauerschmidt et al. [3] for modern expositions on the subject and applications.

By means of the transfer-current matrix, this result can be further expanded to yield an explicit expression of the joint moments of the fields \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\big (X_v^{(k_v)} Y_v\big )_v$$\end{document}(Xv(kv)Yv)v in terms of the Green’s function of the graph \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal G$$\end{document}G, given in Theorem 4.2. To the best of the author knowledge, in the literature there is no full general expression for the exact distribution of the degree field of a UST on a general graph. This can be applied, for example, to calculate the probability of a vertex on the complete graph \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$K_n$$\end{document}Kn to have degree k, for any \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$n\ge 1$$\end{document}n≥1. Taking \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$n\rightarrow \infty $$\end{document}n→∞, we show that the degree variable behaves as a Poisson variable plus 1, a result which was already known [2, 16], but it comes in a more straight-forward manner with our approach since we have an explicit expression of the probability mass function of the degree variable for any \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$n\ge 1$$\end{document}n≥1 at any given point.

The constants \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$C_\textbf{L}^{(k_v)}$$\end{document}CL(kv) are explicitly calculated in terms of the Green’s function values of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textbf{L}$$\end{document}L. We observe that the expression for the limiting cumulants are the same for all lattices up to a constant, hinting towards a potential universality property of the system. Unlike in Cipriani et al. [6], the proof of this now more general limiting result is unified for all the lattices considered, which makes the necessary conditions of the lattice more clear for our proof to work. The reader will also observe that expression (1.2) has exactly the same functional form as that of the height-one field of the ASM [6, 7], albeit with a different constant in front, meaning that the limiting joint cumulants expressions are affected by the values of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(k_v)_v$$\end{document}(kv)v only through \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$C_{\textbf{L}}^{(k_v)}$$\end{document}CL(kv), but otherwise remain the same.

Structure of the paper. We begin our paper setting up notation and defining the main objects of interest in Section 2. Section 3 is devoted to recapitulate on the fermionic formalism used throughout the paper, as well as stating the first general results linking fermionic Gaussian states and UST probabilities. The moments/cumulants, both for a finite graph and the limiting case, are in Section 4. At the end of that section we also discuss the case of the complete graph \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$K_n$$\end{document}Kn and its limit \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$n\rightarrow \infty $$\end{document}n→∞. Finally, Section 5 is devoted to the proofs of the main theorems.

Notation and Definitions

Lattices, sets and functions For the rest of the paper d will denote the dimension of the underlying space we work on. We will write |A| for the cardinality of a set A. For \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$n\in \mathbb {N}$$\end{document}n∈N, let [n] denote the set \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\{1,\,\ldots ,\,n\}$$\end{document}{1,…,n}.

Throughout the paper \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textbf{L}$$\end{document}L will denote a lattice. In particular, we will consider what we will call the hypercubic lattice \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {Z}^d$$\end{document}Zd, the two dimensional triangular lattice \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textbf{T}$$\end{document}T, and we will also make some remarks on the two dimensional hexagonal lattice \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textbf{H}$$\end{document}H. Since these lattices are regular, we write \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\deg _{\textbf{L}}$$\end{document}degL for the degree of any vertex, which is 2d for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {Z}^d$$\end{document}Zd, 6 for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textbf{T}$$\end{document}T, and 3 for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textbf{H}$$\end{document}H.

We will denote an oriented edge f on the lattice \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textbf{L}$$\end{document}L as the ordered pair \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(f^-,\,f^+)$$\end{document}(f-,f+), being \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$f^-$$\end{document}f- the tail and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$f^+$$\end{document}f+ the tip of the edge. Denote \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\{e_i\}_{i\in [\deg _{\textbf{L}}]}$$\end{document}{ei}i∈[degL] the set of edges with tail in the origin. The \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$e_i$$\end{document}ei’s define a natural orientation of edges which we will tacitly choose whenever we need oriented edges (for example when defining the matrix M in (2.5)). The collection of all \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$e_i$$\end{document}ei, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$i\in \{1,\,\ldots ,\,\deg _{\textbf{L}}\}$$\end{document}i∈{1,…,degL}, will be called \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$E_o$$\end{document}Eo, where o is the origin. By abuse of notation but convenient for the paper, if \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$f=(f^-,\,f^-+e_i)$$\end{document}f=(f-,f-+ei) for some \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$i\in \left[ \deg _{\textbf{L}}\right] $$\end{document}i∈degL, we denote by \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$-f$$\end{document}-f the edge \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(f^-,\,f^- - e_i)$$\end{document}(f-,f--ei) whenever it exists; that is, the reflection of f over \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$f^-$$\end{document}f-.

Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$A \subseteq \mathbb {R}^d$$\end{document}A⊆Rd be a countable set. For every \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$v \in A$$\end{document}v∈A, denote by \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$E_v$$\end{document}Ev the set \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$E_o+v$$\end{document}Eo+v, and let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$E(A) = \bigcup _{v\in A} E_v$$\end{document}E(A)=⋃v∈AEv.

(Discrete Laplacian on a graph) We define the (unnormalized) discrete Laplacian on \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textbf{L}$$\end{document}L as2.1where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$u,\,v \in \textbf{L}$$\end{document}u,v∈L and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$u \sim v$$\end{document}u∼v denotes that u and v are nearest neighbors. Note that we define the function taking values in an algebra because we will apply the Laplacian both on real-valued functions and functions defined on Grassmannian algebras, which will be introduced in Section 3.

Cumulants We now give a brief recap of the definition of cumulants and joint cumulants for random variables. Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$n\in \mathbb {N}$$\end{document}n∈N and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textbf{X}=(X_{i})_{i=1}^n$$\end{document}X=(Xi)i=1n be a vector of real-valued random variables, each of which has all finite moments.

Definition 2.5

(Joint cumulants of random vector) The cumulant generating function \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} as\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ K(\textbf{t}) {:=}\log \left( \mathbb {E}\big [e^{ \textbf{t}\cdot \textbf{X}}\big ]\right) = \sum _{\textbf{m}\in \mathbb {N}^n} \kappa _{\textbf{m}}(\textbf{X}) \prod _{j=1}^n \frac{t_j^{m_j}}{m_j!} \ , $$\end{document}K(t):=logE[et·X]=∑m∈Nnκm(X)∏j=1ntjjmj!,where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textbf{t}\cdot \textbf{X}$$\end{document}t·X denotes the scalar product in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {R}^n$$\end{document}Rn, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textbf{m}=(m_1,\,\dots ,\,m_n)\in \mathbb {N}^n$$\end{document}m=(m1,⋯,mn)∈Nn is a multi-index with n components, and\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ \kappa _{\textbf{m}}(\textbf{X})=\frac{\partial ^{|m|}}{\partial t_1^{m_1}\cdots \partial t_n^{m_n}}K(\textbf{t})\Big |_{t_1=\ldots =t_n=0} \ , $$\end{document}κm(X)=∂|m|∂t11⋯∂tnnK(t)|t1=…=tn=0,being \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$|m|=m_1+\cdots +m_n$$\end{document}|m|=m1+⋯+mn. The joint cumulant of the components of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textbf{X}$$\end{document}X can be defined as a Taylor coefficient of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$K(t_1,\,\ldots ,\,t_n)$$\end{document}K(t1,…,tn) for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textbf{m}=(1,\,\ldots ,\,1)$$\end{document}m=(1,…,1); in other words\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ \kappa (X_1,\,\ldots ,\,X_n)=\frac{\partial ^n}{\partial t_1\cdots \partial t_n} K(\textbf{t})\Big |_{t_1=\ldots =t_n=0} \ . $$\end{document}κ(X1,…,Xn)=∂n∂t1⋯∂tnK(t)|t1=…=tn=0.In particular, for any \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$A\subseteq [n]$$\end{document}A⊆[n], the joint cumulant \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\kappa (X_i:\, i\in A)$$\end{document}κ(Xi:i∈A) of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textbf{X}$$\end{document}X can be computed as\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ \kappa (X_i:\, i\in A) = \sum _{\pi \in \Pi (A)} (|\pi |-1)! (-1)^{|\pi |-1} \prod _{B\in \pi } \mathbb {E} \left[ \prod _{i\in B} X_i \right] \ , $$\end{document}κ(Xi:i∈A)=∑π∈Π(A)(|π|-1)!(-1)|π|-1∏B∈πE∏i∈BXi,with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$|\pi |$$\end{document}|π| the cardinality of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\pi $$\end{document}π.

Let us remark that, by some straightforward combinatorics, it follows from the previous definition that2.4\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \mathbb {E} \left[ \prod _{i\in A} X_i \right] = \sum _{\pi \in \Pi (A)} \prod _{B\in \pi } \kappa (X_i:\, i\in B) \ . \end{aligned}$$\end{document}E∏i∈AXi=∑π∈Π(A)∏B∈πκ(Xi:i∈B).If \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$A=\{i,\,j\}$$\end{document}A={i,j}, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$i,\,j\in [n]$$\end{document}i,j∈[n], then the joint cumulant \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\kappa (X_i,\,X_j)$$\end{document}κ(Xi,Xj) is the covariance between \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$X_{i}$$\end{document}Xi and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$X_{j}$$\end{document}Xj. We stress that, for a real-valued random variable X, one has the equality\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ \kappa (\underbrace{X,\,\ldots ,\,X}_{n\text { times}})=\kappa _n(X),\quad n\in \mathbb {N}, $$\end{document}κ(X,…,X⏟ntimes)=κn(X),n∈N,which we call the n-th cumulant of X.

Good sets and transfer-current matrix We need to introduce a technical requirement for the sets we will study in the theorems that follow, that prevents us from choosing points that share edges. This requirement can however be circumvented, as we show in Section 4.

Definition 2.6

(Good set) We call \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$A \subseteq \Lambda $$\end{document}A⊆Λ a good set if it does not contain any nearest neighbors. That is, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$||v-u|| > 1$$\end{document}||v-u||>1 for any \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$u,\,v\in A$$\end{document}u,v∈A.

Finally, we need the notion of the transfer-current matrix, a key ingredient in many expressions we obtain in our theorems.

Definition 2.7

(Transfer-current matrix) We define the transfer-current matrix \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$M_\Lambda $$\end{document}MΛ as2.5\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} M_\Lambda (f,\,g) {:=}\nabla _{\eta ^*(f)}^{(1)}\nabla _{\eta ^*(g)}^{(2)}G_{\Lambda }(f^{-},\,g^-) ,\quad f,\,g \in E(\Lambda ), \end{aligned}$$\end{document}MΛ(f,g):=∇η∗(f)(1)∇η∗(g)(2)GΛ(f-,g-),f,g∈E(Λ),where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\eta ^*(f)\in E_o$$\end{document}η∗(f)∈Eo is the coordinate direction induced by \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$f\in E(\Lambda )$$\end{document}f∈E(Λ) on \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$f^-$$\end{document}f- (in the sense that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\eta ^*(f)=e_i$$\end{document}η∗(f)=ei if \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$f=(f^-,\, f^-+e_i)$$\end{document}f=(f-,f-+ei)). Hereafter, to simplify notation we will omit the dependence of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$M_\Lambda $$\end{document}MΛ on \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Lambda $$\end{document}Λ and simply write M.

Remark 1

As stated in Lyons and Peres [13], there is another interpretation of M in terms of electrical networks, as follows: let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal G$$\end{document}G represent an electric network with impedance 1 on each edge. Defining \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\phi _{f}(x)$$\end{document}ϕf(x) as the voltage at vertex \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$x\in \Lambda $$\end{document}x∈Λ when a battery of 1 volt is connected between vertices \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$g^-$$\end{document}g- and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$g^+$$\end{document}g+ by removing the resistance on g and setting the voltage at \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$f^-$$\end{document}f- to 0, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$M(f,\,g)$$\end{document}M(f,g) is given by\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ M = \phi _f(g^+) - \phi _f(g^-) . $$\end{document}M=ϕf(g+)-ϕf(g-).

Fermionic Formalism

As we will see in this section, working with Grassmann variables (i.e., fermions) allow us to express properties from the UST. We will not give a complete exposition of the subject in this paper; however, the interested reader can resort to Cipriani et al. [6] for a similar setting to the one used here, or Abdesselam [1], Meyer [15] for a more comprehensive presentation.

Definition 3.1

(Abdesselam [1, Definition 1]) Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$M\in \mathbb {N}$$\end{document}M∈N and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\xi _1,\,\ldots ,\,\xi _M$$\end{document}ξ1,…,ξM be a collection of letters. Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {R}\left[ \xi _{1},\,\ldots ,\,\xi _{M}\right] $$\end{document}Rξ1,…,ξM be the quotient of the free non-commutative algebra \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {R}\langle \xi _1,\,\ldots ,\,\xi _M\rangle $$\end{document}R⟨ξ1,…,ξM⟩ by the two-sided ideal generated by the anticommutation relations3.1\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \xi _j \xi _j = - \xi _i \xi _j, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}ξjξj=-ξiξj,where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$i,\,j \in [M]$$\end{document}i,j∈[M]. We will denote it by \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Omega ^{M}$$\end{document}ΩM and call it the Grassmann algebra in M variables. The \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\xi $$\end{document}ξ’s will be referred to as Grassmannian variables or generators. Due to anticommutation these variables are called “fermionic” (as opposed to commutative or “bosonic” variables).

Notice that, due to the anticommutative property, we have that for any variable Pauli’s exclusion principle holds [1, Proposition 2]:3.2\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \xi _i^2 = 0, \quad i\in [M]. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}ξi2=0,i∈[M].

Definition 3.2

(Grassmannian–Berezin integration) The Grassmann–Berezin integral is defined as\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ \int F d \xi {:=}\partial _{\xi _{M}}\partial _{\xi _{M-1}} \cdots \partial _{\xi _2}\partial _{\xi _1}F, \quad F\in \Omega ^{M}. $$\end{document}∫Fdξ:=∂ξM∂ξM-1⋯∂ξ2∂ξ1F,F∈ΩM.

On the grounds of this definition, for the rest of the paper Grassmannian–Berezin integrals will be denoted by \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\left( \prod _{i=1}^M\partial _{\xi _i}\right) F$$\end{document}∏i=1M∂ξiF.

The most important result that we will use in this work is as follows. For a given matrix \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$A = (A_{i,\,j})_{i \in I_0,\, j \in J_0}$$\end{document}A=(Ai,j)i∈I0,j∈J0, and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$I \subseteq I_0,\, J\subseteq J_0$$\end{document}I⊆I0,J⊆J0, such that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$|I|=|J|$$\end{document}|I|=|J|, we write \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\det (A)_{IJ}$$\end{document}det(A)IJ to denote the determinant of the submatrix \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(A_{i,\,j})_{i \in I,\, j \in J}$$\end{document}(Ai,j)i∈I,j∈J. When \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$I=J$$\end{document}I=J, we simply write \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\det (A)_I$$\end{document}det(A)I.

Theorem 3.3

(Wick’s theorem for “complex” fermions) Let A be an \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${m\times m}$$\end{document}m×m, B an \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ {r\times m}$$\end{document}r×m and C an \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ {m\times r}$$\end{document}m×r matrix respectively with coefficients in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {R}$$\end{document}R. For any sequences of indices \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$I = \{i_1,\,\dots ,\,i_r\}$$\end{document}I={i1,⋯,ir} and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$J = \{j_1,\,\dots ,\,j_r\}$$\end{document}J={j1,⋯,jr} in [m] of the same length r, if the matrix A is invertible we have \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\displaystyle \left( \prod _{i=1}^m\partial _{\overline{\psi }_i}\partial _{\psi _i}\right) \prod _{\alpha =1}^r \psi _{i_\alpha }\overline{\psi }_{j_\alpha }\exp \left( (\varvec{\psi },\,A\varvec{\overline{\psi }})\right) = \det (A) \det \left( A^{-\intercal \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\displaystyle \left( \prod _{i=1}^m\partial _{\overline{\psi }_i}\partial _{\psi _i}\right) \prod _{\alpha =1}^r (\psi ^T C)_\alpha (B\overline{\psi })_\alpha \exp \left( (\varvec{\psi },\,A\varvec{\overline{\psi }})\right) = \det (A) \det \left( BA^{-1}C\right) \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$|I| \ne |J|$$\end{document}|I|≠|J|, the integral is 0.

\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\displaystyle \left( \prod _{i=1}^m\partial _{\overline{\psi }_i}\partial _{\psi _i}\right) \prod _{\alpha =1}^r \psi _{i_\alpha }\overline{\psi }_{j_\alpha }\exp \left( (\varvec{\psi },\,A\varvec{\overline{\psi }})\right) = \det (A) \det \left( A^{-\intercal }\right) _{IJ}$$\end{document}∏i=1m∂ψ¯i∂ψi∏α=1rψiαψ¯jαexp(ψ,Aψ¯)=det(A)detA-⊺IJ,

\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\displaystyle \left( \prod _{i=1}^m\partial _{\overline{\psi }_i}\partial _{\psi _i}\right) \prod _{\alpha =1}^r \psi _{i_\alpha }\overline{\psi }_{j_\alpha }\exp \left( (\varvec{\psi },\,A\varvec{\overline{\psi }})\right) = \det (A) \det \left( A^{-\intercal }\right) _{IJ}$$\end{document}∏i=1m∂ψ¯i∂ψi∏α=1rψiαψ¯jαexp(ψ,Aψ¯)=det(A)detA-⊺IJ,

Definition 3.4

(Fermionic Gaussian free field) The normalized fermionic Gaussian free field state is the linear map \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\left\langle \cdot \right\rangle :\, \Omega ^{2\Lambda } \rightarrow \mathbb {R}$$\end{document}·:Ω2Λ→R defined as\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ \left\langle F\right\rangle {:=}\frac{1}{\det (-\Delta _\Lambda )} \left( \prod _{v\in \Lambda }\partial _{\overline{\psi }_v}\partial _{\psi _v}\right) \exp \left( \langle \varvec{\psi },\,-\Delta _\Lambda \varvec{\overline{\psi }}\rangle \right) F,\quad F\in \Omega ^{2\Lambda }. $$\end{document}F:=1det(-ΔΛ)∏v∈Λ∂ψ¯v∂ψvexp⟨ψ,-ΔΛψ¯⟩F,F∈Ω2Λ.

Fermions and the Uniform Spanning Tree

Now that we have the key ingredients to work with fermions, we will see how these objects allow us to study probabilistic behaviors of the edges of a realization of the UST measure.

We will first consider gradients of the generators in the following sense:

Definition 3.5

(Gradient of the generators) The gradient of the generators in the i-th direction is given by\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ \nabla _{e_i}\psi (v)=\psi _{v+e_i}-\psi _v,\quad \nabla _{e_i}\overline{\psi }(v)=\overline{\psi }_{v+e_i}-\overline{\psi }_v,\quad v\in \Lambda ,\,i=1,\,\ldots ,\,\deg _\mathcal {G}(v). $$\end{document}∇eiψ(v)=ψv+ei-ψv,∇eiψ¯(v)=ψ¯v+ei-ψ¯v,v∈Λ,i=1,…,degG(v).

Define \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\zeta (e)$$\end{document}ζ(e) as\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ \zeta (e) {:=}\nabla _e\psi (e^-)\nabla _e\overline{\psi }(e^+), $$\end{document}ζ(e):=∇eψ(e-)∇eψ¯(e+),and observe that the elements \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\zeta (\cdot )$$\end{document}ζ(·) are commutative, that is,\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ \zeta (a)\zeta (b) = \zeta (b)\zeta (a),\quad \forall \, a,\,b \in E, $$\end{document}ζ(a)ζ(b)=ζ(b)ζ(a),∀a,b∈E,but we still have that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\zeta (a)^2 = 0$$\end{document}ζ(a)2=0. These objects are key when analyzing probabilities of edges showing up in the UST, in the sense of the result that follows.

Proposition 3.6

Let the tree T be a realization of the UST measure. For \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$F,\,G \subseteq E$$\end{document}F,G⊆E, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$F\cap G = \emptyset $$\end{document}F∩G=∅ it holds that\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ \mathbb {P}\left( F \subseteq T,\, G\cap T = \emptyset \right) = \left\langle \prod _{f\in F} \zeta (f) \prod _{g\in G} \left( 1 - \zeta (g)\right) \right\rangle = \det \big (M^{(|F|)}\big ), $$\end{document}PF⊆T,G∩T=∅=∏f∈Fζ(f)∏g∈G1-ζ(g)=det(M(|F|)),where

Proof

Observe that\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ \prod _{g\in G}(1-\zeta (g)) = \sum _{\gamma \subseteq G}(-1)^{|\gamma |}\prod _{g\in \gamma }\zeta (g), $$\end{document}∏g∈G(1-ζ(g))=∑γ⊆G(-1)|γ|∏g∈γζ(g),so that\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \left\langle \prod _{f\in F} \zeta (f) \prod _{g\in G}(1-\zeta (g))\right\rangle= & \sum _{\gamma \subseteq G}(-1)^{|\gamma |} \left\langle \prod _{g\in \gamma }\zeta (g) \prod _{f\in F}\zeta (f)\right\rangle \\= & \sum _{\gamma \subseteq G} (-1)^{|\gamma |} \,\mathbb {P}\left( F \subseteq T,\,\gamma \subseteq T\right) . \end{aligned}$$\end{document}∏f∈Fζ(f)∏g∈G(1-ζ(g))=∑γ⊆G(-1)|γ|∏g∈γζ(g)∏f∈Fζ(f)=∑γ⊆G(-1)|γ|PF⊆T,γ⊆T.Using the inclusion-exclusion principle, we obtain the first equality. The equality between the first and third members follows from Pemantle [16, Theorem 4.3] (noting that there is a typo in their definition of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$M^{(|F|)}$$\end{document}M(|F|)). \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\square $$\end{document}□

Remark 2

In view of Proposition 3.6 we have the following recipe to cook up a field whose expectation matches that of the UST: for each edge f we want in the UST, add a factor \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\zeta (f)$$\end{document}ζ(f), and for each edge g we do not want, add a factor \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$1-\zeta (g)$$\end{document}1-ζ(g). Observe that, once we add an edge e by adding the factor \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\zeta (e)$$\end{document}ζ(e), then adding another factor \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$1-\zeta (e)$$\end{document}1-ζ(e) does nothing. This is easily seen from the fact that\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ \zeta (e) \left( 1-\zeta (e)\right) = \zeta (e) - \zeta (e)^2 = \zeta (e) . $$\end{document}ζ(e)1-ζ(e)=ζ(e)-ζ(e)2=ζ(e).

Degree of the Uniform Spanning Tree

So far we have seen the relationships between fermionic variables and particular edges on a spanning tree. We will now use those results to study the behaviour of the degree of a realization of the UST measure at given points on the graph.

Remark 3

Until now we have defined edges on graphs to be oriented. However, in the following definitions the orientation play no rôle, so we will consider edges as non oriented.

Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal G = (\Lambda ,\,E)$$\end{document}G=(Λ,E) be any graph. For each \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$v\in \Lambda $$\end{document}v∈Λ and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$k_v \in \{1,\,\dots ,\,\deg _{\mathcal {G}}(v)\}$$\end{document}kv∈{1,⋯,degG(v)}, we define the field \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbf {X^{(k)}} = \big (X_v^{(k_v)}\big )_{v\in V}$$\end{document}X(k)=(Xv(kv))v∈V as3.3\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} X_v^{(k_v)}{:=}\sum _{\mathcal {E}\subseteq E_v:\, |\mathcal {E}|=k_v} \prod _{e\in \mathcal {E}} \zeta (e),\quad v\in \Lambda . \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Xv(kv):=∑E⊆Ev:|E|=kv∏e∈Eζ(e),v∈Λ.In view of Remark 2, this is equivalent to asking that the degree of the UST at a point v is at least \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$k_v$$\end{document}kv; that is,If \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$k_v=1$$\end{document}kv=1 for all v, this is just the field \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(X_v)_v$$\end{document}(Xv)v defined in Cipriani et al. [6]. Observe also that, because of the nilpotency property of fermions,\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ X^{(k_v)}_v = (X_v)^{k_v}, $$\end{document}Xv(kv)=(Xv)kv,so we will sometimes indistinctly denote it as \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$X_v^{k_v}$$\end{document}Xvv The same applies for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textbf{X}^{(\textbf{k})}$$\end{document}X(k) written as \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbf {X^k}$$\end{document}Xk. We will also need auxiliary Grassmannian observables \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textbf{Y}=(Y_v)_{v\in V}$$\end{document}Y=(Yv)v∈V given by3.4\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} Y_v{:=}\prod _{e\in E_v}\left( 1-\zeta (e)\right) ,\quad v\in \Lambda . \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Yv:=∏e∈Ev1-ζ(e),v∈Λ.Define the degree field of the UST \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(D_v)_{v\in \Lambda }$$\end{document}(Dv)v∈Λ as3.5which is “equal” (in the sense of its finite-dimensional distributions) to \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\big (X_v\big )_v$$\end{document}(Xv)v, as it was seen in Cipriani et al. [6]. More precisely, for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$V\subseteq \Lambda $$\end{document}V⊆Λ a good set (neighboring points will be dealt with in Section 4),\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ \mathbb {E}\left[ \prod _{v\in V}D_v\right] = \left\langle \prod _{v\in V}X_v\right\rangle . $$\end{document}E∏v∈VDv=∏v∈VXv.For \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$k_v\in \{1,\,\dots ,\,\deg _\mathcal {G}(v)\}$$\end{document}kv∈{1,⋯,degG(v)}, define the degree-\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$k_v$$\end{document}kv field asAs a consequence of Proposition 3.6, we can express the probability of the degree of the UST at different non-neighboring points as follows:

Theorem 3.7

Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$V\subset \Lambda $$\end{document}V⊂Λ be a good set. For any \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$k_v\in \{1,\,\dots ,\,\deg _\mathcal {G}(v)\}$$\end{document}kv∈{1,⋯,degG(v)}, with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$v\in V$$\end{document}v∈V, it holds that3.6\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \mathbb {P}\left( D_v = k_v , \, v\in V\right) = \mathbb {E}\left[ \prod _{v\in V}\delta ^{(k_v)}_v\right] = \left\langle \prod _{v\in V} X_v^{k_v} Y_v\right\rangle . \end{aligned}$$\end{document}PDv=kv,v∈V=E∏v∈Vδv(kv)=∏v∈VXvvYv.

Note that this is a generalization of Cipriani et al. [6, Theorem 3.1], where we obtain the same result for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$k_v=1$$\end{document}kv=1 for all \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$v\in V$$\end{document}v∈V, even though in that case our main focus was the height-one field of the Abelian sandpile model.

Remark 4

Observe that points in V need to be different. In fact, for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$v\in V$$\end{document}v∈V,\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ \mathbb {E}\left[ D_v^2\right] \ne \left\langle X_v^2\right\rangle , $$\end{document}EDv2≠Xv2,and of course neither does it hold for larger powers. This is because the square of an indicator function (see (3.5)) is the same indicator, whereas the square of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\zeta (e)$$\end{document}ζ(e), \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$e\in E$$\end{document}e∈E (see (3.3)), is 0. However, using Proposition 3.6 we observe that\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ \left\langle X_v(X_v+1)\right\rangle = \left\langle X_v^2\right\rangle + \left\langle X_v\right\rangle = \sum _{\begin{array}{c} e,\,f\in E_v\\ e\ne f \end{array}}\det {(M)}_{e,\,f} + \sum _{e\in E_v} M(e,\,e) = \mathbb {E}\left[ D_v^2\right] . $$\end{document}Xv(Xv+1)=Xv2+Xv=∑e,f∈Eve≠fdet(M)e,f+∑e∈EvM(e,e)=EDv2.Following the same reasoning,\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ \mathbb {E}\big [D_v^m\big ] = \sum _{i\in [m]} a_i \left\langle X_v^i\right\rangle , $$\end{document}E[Dvm]=∑i∈[m]aiXvi,where the coefficients \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$a_i$$\end{document}ai correspond to a modification of the binomial coefficients. More precisely, for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ a_i = \left( {\begin{array}{c}m\\ i-1\end{array}}\right) , $$\end{document}ai=mi-1,while for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ a_i = \left( {\begin{array}{c}m\\ i\end{array}}\right) . $$\end{document}ai=mi.We could also find the reverse expression, that is, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\left\langle X_v^m\right\rangle $$\end{document}Xvm as a function of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {E}[D_v^i]$$\end{document}E[Dvi], \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$i\in [m]$$\end{document}i∈[m]. We can use the results on Pemantle [16, Section 5.2] to obtain\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ \left\langle X_v^m\right\rangle = m!\, \mathbb {E}\left[ \left( {\begin{array}{c}D_v\\ m\end{array}}\right) \right] = \mathbb {E}\left[ \prod _{i=0}^{m-1}(D_v-i)\right] $$\end{document}Xvm=m!EDvm=E∏i=0m-1(Dv-i)for any \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$m\in \mathbb {N}$$\end{document}m∈N.

Cumulants of the UST Degree

We will now study the cumulants (related to moments, as seen in Section 2) of the fields \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbf{X^k \, Y}$$\end{document}XkY on an arbitrary graph, and then obtain limiting expressions for some particular lattices. The next theorem is a generalization of Cipriani et al. [6, Theorem 3.5] when \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$k_v=1$$\end{document}kv=1 for all points \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$v\in V$$\end{document}v∈V. Before that, let us define some key elements for our theorems.

General definitions. Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Lambda $$\end{document}Λ be a finite and connected (in the usual graph sense) subset of a lattice \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textbf{L}$$\end{document}L and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$V \subseteq \Lambda $$\end{document}V⊆Λ be a good set according to Definition 2.6. As V is a good set, notice that every edge in E(V) is connected to exactly one vertex in V.

For any finite set A we denote the set of permutations of A by S(A). Furthermore, we write \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$S_{{{\,\textrm{cycl}\,}}}(A)$$\end{document}Scycl(A) to denote the set of cyclic permutations of A (without fixed points).

Permutations: connected and bare. We define the multigraph \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$V_\tau = \left( V,\, E_\tau (V)\right) $$\end{document}Vτ=V,Eτ(V) induced by \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\tau $$\end{document}τ in the following way. For each pair of vertices \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$v \ne w$$\end{document}v≠w in V, we add one edge between v and w for each \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$f \in E_v, f' \in E_w$$\end{document}f∈Ev,f′∈Ew such that either \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\tau (f)= f'$$\end{document}τ(f)=f′ or \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\tau (f')= f$$\end{document}τ(f′)=f. If \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$v=w$$\end{document}v=w, we add no edge, so \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\deg _{V_\tau }(v) \le |E_v|$$\end{document}degVτ(v)≤|Ev|.

Fix \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$A\subseteq E(V)$$\end{document}A⊆E(V) such that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$E_v \cap A \ne \emptyset $$\end{document}Ev∩A≠∅ for all \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$v \in V$$\end{document}v∈V, i.e. we have a set of edges with at least one edge per vertex of V. Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\tau \in S(A)$$\end{document}τ∈S(A) be a permutation of edges in A.

Definition 4.1

(Connected and bare permutations) Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} We are now ready to state the theorems. Let U be a bounded, open, connected subset of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {R}^d$$\end{document}Rd with smooth boundary, and define \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$U_\varepsilon {:=}U/\varepsilon \cap \textbf{L}$$\end{document}Uε:=U/ε∩L for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\varepsilon >0$$\end{document}ε>0. Define \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$g_U$$\end{document}gU as the continuum harmonic Green’s function on U with 0-boundary conditions outside U. We write \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$X_v^\varepsilon $$\end{document}Xvε, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textbf{X}^\varepsilon $$\end{document}Xε and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$Y_v^\varepsilon $$\end{document}Yvε, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textbf{Y}^\varepsilon $$\end{document}Yε, with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$v\in U$$\end{document}v∈U, to emphasize that the domain is now on \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$U_\varepsilon $$\end{document}Uε, so that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$X^\varepsilon _v = X_{v_\varepsilon }$$\end{document}Xvε=Xvε, and analogously with the other variables.

Theorem 4.2

(Cumulants of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\mathbf {X^k} \, \textbf{Y}}$$\end{document}XkY on a graph) Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal G = (\Lambda ,\,E)$$\end{document}G=(Λ,E) be any graph.
