Remark 5

The reader might be wondering why we work with cumulants instead of moments in this case, which in view of Theorem 3.7, seems to only introduce complications. The reason for this is that cumulants are independent of the mean, which allows us to obtain a limiting result in the next theorem without the need of renormalizing.

Let us now turn our attention to homogeneous lattices \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textbf{L}$$\end{document}L. Specifically, we will consider the triangular lattice \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textbf{T}$$\end{document}T and the hexagonal lattice \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textbf{H}$$\end{document}H in 2 dimensions, as well as the hypercubic lattice \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb Z^d$$\end{document}Zd in d dimensions, for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$d \ge 2$$\end{document}d≥2. Take any two non-parallel edges incident on the same vertex v, for any \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$v\in V$$\end{document}v∈V, and consider the plane spanned by these two edges. Let p be the number of edges incident on the same v which are contained in this plane, and let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\alpha \in \{0,\,\dots ,\,p - 1\}$$\end{document}α∈{0,⋯,p-1}; that is, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\alpha = 4$$\end{document}α=4 for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb Z^d$$\end{document}Zd, 6 for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textbf{T}$$\end{document}T and 3 for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textbf{H}$$\end{document}H. Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\gamma _\alpha {:=}\cos \left( 2\pi \alpha /p\right) $$\end{document}γα:=cos2πα/p. This next theorem is a generalization of Cipriani et al. [6, Theorems 3.6 and 5.1.2] when \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$k_v=1$$\end{document}kv=1 for all \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$v\in V$$\end{document}v∈V. We unify their statements and proofs in one theorem.

Theorem 4.3

(Scaling limit of the cumulants of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbf {X^{k} \, Y}$$\end{document}XkY) Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$n\ge 2$$\end{document}n≥2, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$V{:=}\left\{ v_1,\,\dots ,\,v_n\right\} \subseteq U$$\end{document}V:=v1,⋯,vn⊆U be such that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${{\,\textrm{dist}\,}}(V,\,\partial U)>0$$\end{document}dist(V,∂U)>0, and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textbf{L}$$\end{document}L the lattice \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {Z}^d$$\end{document}Zd or \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textbf{T}$$\end{document}T. Remark 6

The reader might have noticed that expression (4.2) can be expressed as a sum of traces of a product of matrices. More precisely,\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ (4.2) = - \left[ \prod _{v\in V}C_\textbf{L}^{(k_v)}\right] \sum _{\sigma \in S_{{{\,\textrm{cycl}\,}}}(V)} \textrm{Tr}\left( \prod _{v\in V} G(v,\, \sigma (v))\right) . $$\end{document}(4.2)=-∏v∈VCL(kv)∑σ∈Scycl(V)Tr∏v∈VG(v,σ(v)).with the matrix \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$G = (G_{i,\,j})_{i,\,j}$$\end{document}G=(Gi,j)i,j as\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ G_{i,\,j}(v,\, \sigma (v)) {:=}\partial _{e_i}^{(1)}\partial _{e_j}^{(2)} g_U\left( v,\, \sigma (v)\right) . $$\end{document}Gi,j(v,σ(v)):=∂ei(1)∂ej(2)gUv,σ(v).

Remark 7

As we will see in the proof, the same techniques are immediately generalizable to the hexagonal lattice; that is, for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textbf{L}= \textbf{H}$$\end{document}L=H. However, that case requires more care, since we have to account for the two types of vertices in that lattice. We believe an adaptation of the proof to that case only adds obscurity to the matter, but nonetheless it can still be done, yielding the same expression with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$p=3$$\end{document}p=3 and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$c_{\textbf{H}} = 3/2$$\end{document}cH=3/2.

Remark 8

After the proof of this theorem, on page 23 we provide a table with the explicit values of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$C_{\textbf{L}}^{(k)}$$\end{document}CL(k) for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {Z}^2$$\end{document}Z2, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textbf{T}$$\end{document}T and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textbf{H}$$\end{document}H, using the potential kernel values of the lattices (see e.g. Kenyon and Wilson [10] or Poncelet and Ruelle [17]). The reader can observe in the table that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$C_{\textbf{H}}^{(2)} = 0$$\end{document}CH(2)=0, which means that any cumulant involving \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$k_v=2$$\end{document}kv=2 at any v automatically vanishes on the hexagonal lattice.

What about neighboring points? A natural question that arises is whether we can relax the good set condition on the set V in theorems 3.7 and 4.2. The answer is yes, as we explain below.

Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal G = (\Lambda ,\,E)$$\end{document}G=(Λ,E) be any graph, T a realization of the UST distribution, and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$v\sim w\in \Lambda $$\end{document}v∼w∈Λ. Then\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \mathbb {P}\left( D_v = k_v,\, D_w = k_w\right) =\\ \mathbb {P}\left( D_v = k_v,\, D_w = k_w,\, \{v,\,w\}\in T\right) + \mathbb {P}\left( D_v = k_v,\, D_w = k_w,\, \{v,\,w\}\notin T\right) . \end{aligned}$$\end{document}PDv=kv,Dw=kw=PDv=kv,Dw=kw,{v,w}∈T+PDv=kv,Dw=kw,{v,w}∉T.As we saw in Remark 2, the condition \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\{v,\,w\}\in T$$\end{document}{v,w}∈T translates, in the fermionic language, to introducing the multiplicative factor \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\zeta (\{v,\,w\})$$\end{document}ζ({v,w}), whereas for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\{v,\,w\}\notin T$$\end{document}{v,w}∉T we need to introduce \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$1-\zeta (\{v,\,w\})$$\end{document}1-ζ({v,w}). In view of Theorem 4.2, we have\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \kappa \left( X_v^{k_v}Y_v,\, X_w^{k_w}Y_w,\, \zeta (\{v,\,w\})\right) = (-1)^{k_v+k_w} \sum _{\begin{array}{c} |\mathcal {E}_v|\ge k_v-1\\ \{v,\,w\}\notin \mathcal {E}_v \end{array}} \, \sum _{\begin{array}{c} |\mathcal {E}_w|\ge k_w-1\\ \{v,\,w\}\notin \mathcal {E}_w \end{array}} (-1)^{|\mathcal S_{{{\,\textrm{co}\,}}}(\mathcal {E})} {{\,\textrm{sign}\,}}(\tau ) \prod _{f\in \mathcal {E}} M\left( f,\,\tau (f)\right) . \end{aligned}$$\end{document}κXvvYv,XwwYw,ζ({v,w})=(-1)kv+kw∑|Ev|≥kv-1{v,w}∉Ev∑|Ew|≥kw-1{v,w}∉Ew(-1)|Ev|+|Ew|×|Ev|kv-1|Ew|kw-1∑τ∈Sco(E)sign(τ)∏f∈EMf,τ(f).Equivalently,\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \kappa \left( X_v^{k_v}Y_v,\, X_w^{k_w}Y_w,\, 1-\zeta (\{v,\,w\})\right) = (-1)^{k_v+k_w} \sum _{\begin{array}{c} |\mathcal {E}_v|\ge k_v\\ \{v,\,w\}\notin \mathcal these expressions we can calculate the moments that give the sought-after probabilities. This is immediately generalized to the case of an arbitrary finite number of points.

Complete graphs It is shown in Pemantle [16, Theorem 1.3] that, for any complete graph \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$K_n$$\end{document}Kn with n vertices, as n goes to infinity the degree of the UST at any vertex v converges in distribution to a random variable \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$1+\mathcal P(1)$$\end{document}1+P(1), being \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal P(1)$$\end{document}P(1) a Poisson variable with parameter 1. This can also be obtained as a corollary from our Theorem 4.2 in a much shorter way, as follows:

Theorem 4.4

(Pemantle [16, Theorem 1.3]) Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$K_n$$\end{document}Kn be a complete graph with n vertices, and let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$V(K_n)$$\end{document}V(Kn) be its vertex set. For any \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$v\in V(K_n)$$\end{document}v∈V(Kn) it holds that\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ D_v \xrightarrow [n\rightarrow \infty ]{\textrm{dist}} 1 + \mathcal P(1), $$\end{document}Dv→n→∞dist1+P(1),with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal P(1)$$\end{document}P(1) a Poisson random variable with parameter 1.

Remark 9

As Pemantle [16, Section 5.2] mentions, this result holds for a more general set of graphs, which the author calls Gino-regular graphs, and the proof follows in the same way. A sequence of graphs \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(\mathcal G_n)_n$$\end{document}(Gn)n is called Gino-regular if there exists a sequence of positive integers \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} This type of graphs allow for an asymptotic calculation of the determinant of M, so that in the limit we obtain the same results as in the case of the complete graph.

Proofs of Theorems 3.7, 4.2 and 4.3

Proof of Theorem 3.7

The first equality is trivial from the fact that for any random variable X and any measurable set A. Let us then prove the second equality, starting with a simple lemma.

Lemma 5.1

The degree-k fields satisfy5.1\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \mathbb {E}\left[ \prod _{v\in V}\delta ^{(k_v)}_v\right] = \sum _{\begin{array}{c} \eta :\,V\rightarrow 2^{E_o}\\ |\eta (v)|= k_v\; \forall v\in V \end{array}} \mathbb {P}\big (\left\{ e \in T \ \forall \, e \in \eta (V)\right\} \, \cap \, \left\{ e' \notin T \ \forall \, e'\in E(V) \setminus \eta (V)\right\} \big ), \end{aligned}$$\end{document}E∏v∈Vδv(kv)=∑η:V→2Eo|η(v)|=kv∀v∈VP(e∈T∀e∈η(V)∩e′∉T∀e′∈E(V)\η(V)),where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\eta (V)$$\end{document}η(V) is an abuse of notation for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\cup _{v\in V}\eta (v)$$\end{document}∪v∈Vη(v).

Proof

This is immediate from the fact that, for any random variable X, any \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal I\subset \mathbb {N}$$\end{document}I⊂N, and measurable sets \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$A_i$$\end{document}Ai with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$i\in \mathcal I$$\end{document}i∈I, . \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\square $$\end{document}□

Before we proceed with the proof, let us recall Proposition 4.4 from Cipriani et al. [6].

Proposition 5.2

Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal G=(\Lambda ,\, E)$$\end{document}G=(Λ,E) be a finite graph. For all subsets of edges \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$S\subseteq E$$\end{document}S⊆E5.2\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \mathbb {P}(T:\, S\subseteq T) = \left\langle \prod _{f\in S}\zeta (f)\right\rangle . \end{aligned}$$\end{document}P(T:S⊆T)=∏f∈Sζ(f).

Proof of Theorem 3.7

In view of Lemma 5.1, take any \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\eta :\, V\rightarrow 2^{E(V)}$$\end{document}η:V→2E(V), with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$|\eta (v)|= k_v$$\end{document}|η(v)|=kv, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$k_v\in \{1,\,\dots ,\,\deg _\mathcal {G}(v)\}$$\end{document}kv∈{1,⋯,degG(v)}, for each \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$v\in V$$\end{document}v∈V. First we observe that\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \bigcap _{v\in V}\left( \left\{ \eta (v)\subseteq T \right\} \cap \left( \bigcup _{e\in E_v\setminus \{\eta (v)\}}\{e\in T\}\right) ^c\right) = \bigcap _{v\in V}\left\{ \eta (v)\subseteq T \right\} \cap \left( \bigcup _{e\in E(V)\setminus \{\eta (V)\}}\{e\in T\}\right) ^c. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}⋂v∈Vη(v)⊆T∩⋃e∈Ev\{η(v)}{e∈T}c=⋂v∈Vη(v)⊆T∩⋃e∈E(V)\{η(V)}{e∈T}c.By the inclusion–exclusion principle,5.3\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \mathbb {P}\left( \bigcap _{v\in V}\left( \left\{ \eta (v)\subseteq T \right\} \cap \left( \bigcup _{e\in E_v\setminus \{\eta (v)\}}\{e\in T\}\right) ^c\right) \right) \nonumber \\ \begin{aligned}&= \mathbb {P}\left( \bigcap _{v\in V}\{\eta (v)\subseteq T \}\right) - By Proposition 5.2, this becomes5.4\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \sum _{S \subseteq E(V) \backslash \eta (V)} (-1)^{|S|} \left\langle \prod _{\{r,\,s\} \in \eta (V)}\zeta (\{r,\,s\})\prod _{\{u,\,w\}\in S}\zeta (\{u,\,w\})\right\rangle . \end{aligned}$$\end{document}∑S⊆E(V)\η(V)(-1)|S|∏{r,s}∈η(V)ζ({r,s})∏{u,w}∈Sζ({u,w}).By the anticommutation relation, the sets of edges S such that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$S \cap \eta (V) \ne \emptyset $$\end{document}S∩η(V)≠∅ do not contribute to (5.4). [6, Theorem 3.5], yielding\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ \kappa \left( Z_v^{(k_v)}:\, v \in V\right) = (-1)^{\sum _v k_v} \sum _{\mathcal {E}:\, |\mathcal {E}_{v}|\ge k_v \; \forall v} K(\mathcal {E}) \sum _{\tau \in S_{{{\,\textrm{co}\,}}}(\mathcal {E})} {{\,\textrm{sign}\,}}(\tau ) \prod _{f\in \mathcal {E}} M\left( f,\,\tau (f)\right) $$\end{document}κZv(kv):v∈V=(-1)∑vkv∑E:|Ev|≥kv∀vK(E)∑τ∈Sco(E)sign(τ)∏f∈EMf,τ(f)as we wanted to show. \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\square $$\end{document}□

Proof of Theorem 4.3

Proof of Theorem 4.3

We will give a proof that works for both \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textbf{L}= \mathbb {Z}^d$$\end{document}L=Zd and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textbf{L}= \textbf{T}$$\end{document}L=T (and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textbf{H}$$\end{document}H with an exception that we will mention below). The proof is divided into four steps. In Step 1, we start from the final expression obtained in Theorem 4.2 and show that it suffices to sum over only bare permutations \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\tau $$\end{document}τ, instead of the bigger set of connected permutations. This is because, in the limit \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\varepsilon \rightarrow 0$$\end{document}ε→0, only bare permutations give non zero contributions. In Step 2, we write the expression in terms of contributions of the permutations acting locally in the vicinity of a vertex and globally mapping an edge incident to one vertex to an edge which is incident to another vertex. In Step 3 we argue that, given a permutation \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\tau $$\end{document}τ on edges and an entry edge for any given point \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$v\in V$$\end{document}v∈V, only the projection of the exit edge onto the entry edge will contribute to the final expression when we take \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\varepsilon \rightarrow 0$$\end{document}ε→0, so we can treat the former as a new edge in the direction of the entry one, weighed by its projection. Finally, in Step 4 we identify the global multiplicative constant of the cumulants. Step 1.From Theorem 4.2 we start with the expression \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ \kappa \left( \left( Z_v^{(k_v)}\right) ^\varepsilon :\, v \in V\right) = (-1)^{\sum _v k_v} \sum _{\mathcal {E}:\, |\mathcal {E}_{v_\varepsilon }|\ge k_v \; \forall v} K(\mathcal {E}) \sum _{\tau \in S_{{{\,\textrm{co}\,}}}(\mathcal {E})} {{\,\textrm{sign}\,}}(\tau ) \prod _{f\in \mathcal {E}} M\left( f,\,\tau (f)\right) . $$\end{document}κZv(kv)ε:v∈V=(-1)∑vkv∑E:|Evε|≥kv∀vK(E)∑τ∈Sco(E)sign(τ)∏f∈EMf,τ(f). This step is practically identical to Step 1 in the proof of Theorem 3.6 in Cipriani et al. [6], since it does not depend on \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$k_v$$\end{document}kv, so we omit the whole derivation. It is obtained that, in the limit \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\varepsilon \rightarrow 0$$\end{document}ε→0, only bare permutations contribute to the final result, obtaining the expression 5.5\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} (-1)^{\sum _v k_v} \sum _{\mathcal {E}:\, |\mathcal {E}_{v}|\ge k_v \; \forall v} K(\mathcal {E}) \sum _{\tau \in S_{{{\,\textrm{bare}\,}}}(\mathcal {E})} {{\,\textrm{sign}\,}}(\tau ) \prod _{f\in \mathcal {E}} \overline{M}\left( f,\,\tau (f)\right) , \end{aligned}$$\end{document}(-1)∑vkv∑E:|Ev|≥kv∀vK(E)∑τ∈Sbare(E)sign(τ)∏f∈EM¯f,τ(f), where we use the notation 5.6 whenever \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\eta ^*(f)=e_i$$\end{document}η∗(f)=ei and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\eta ^*(\tau (f)) = e_j$$\end{document}η∗(τ(f))=ej for some \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$e_i,\,e_j\in E_o$$\end{document}ei,ej∈Eo.

From Theorem 4.2 we start with the expression \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ \kappa \left( \left( Z_v^{(k_v)}\right) ^\varepsilon :\, v \in V\right) = (-1)^{\sum _v k_v} \sum _{\mathcal {E}:\, |\mathcal {E}_{v_\varepsilon }|\ge k_v \; \forall v} K(\mathcal {E}) \sum _{\tau \in S_{{{\,\textrm{co}\,}}}(\mathcal {E})} {{\,\textrm{sign}\,}}(\tau ) \prod _{f\in \mathcal {E}} M\left( f,\,\tau (f)\right) . $$\end{document}κZv(kv)ε:v∈V=(-1)∑vkv∑E:|Evε|≥kv∀vK(E)∑τ∈Sco(E)sign(τ)∏f∈EMf,τ(f). Remark 10

In the hexagonal lattice there are two types of points: those with edges at 0, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$2\pi /3$$\end{document}2π/3 and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$4\pi /3$$\end{document}4π/3 degrees, and those with edges at \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\pi /3$$\end{document}π/3, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\pi $$\end{document}π and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$5\pi /6$$\end{document}5π/6 degrees. Following the proof in Cipriani et al. [6], this step needs extra care when dealing with the hexagonal lattice, since as \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\varepsilon \rightarrow 0$$\end{document}ε→0, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$v_\varepsilon $$\end{document}vε alternates between the two different types of points. Nevertheless, regardless of the point, the contribution will be the same and the result holds for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textbf{H}$$\end{document}H as well, but we omit this technical detail.

Step 2.Given \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\tau \in S_{{{\,\textrm{bare}\,}}}(\mathcal {E})$$\end{document}τ∈Sbare(E), fix \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$v\in V$$\end{document}v∈V, and let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\eta (v)=\eta (v,\,\tau )$$\end{document}η(v)=η(v,τ) be the edge through which \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\tau $$\end{document}τ enters v. Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\gamma _\alpha (v) {:=}\cos \left( 2\pi \alpha (v)/p\right) $$\end{document}γα(v):=cos2πα(v)/p, so that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ \langle \eta (v),\,\eta ^\alpha (v)\rangle = \gamma _\alpha (v). $$\end{document}⟨η(v),ηα(v)⟩=γα(v). Given \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\tau \in S_{{{\,\textrm{bare}\,}}}(\mathcal {E})$$\end{document}τ∈Sbare(E), fix \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$v\in V$$\end{document}v∈V, and let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\eta (v)=\eta (v,\,\tau )$$\end{document}η(v)=η(v,τ) be the edge through which \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\tau $$\end{document}τ enters v. We define \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\eta ^\alpha (v)$$\end{document}ηα(v) as the edge through which \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\tau $$\end{document}τ exists v, and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$2\pi \alpha (v)/p$$\end{document}2πα(v)/p denotes the angle between the entry and exit edges. Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\gamma _\alpha (v) {:=}\cos \left( 2\pi \alpha (v)/p\right) $$\end{document}γα(v):=cos2πα(v)/p, so that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ \langle \eta (v),\,\eta ^\alpha (v)\rangle = \gamma _\alpha (v). $$\end{document}⟨η(v),ηα(v)⟩=γα(v). In the case of the hypercubic lattice the angles between entry and exit edges are multiples of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\pi /2$$\end{document}π/2, hence their cosines belong to \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\{-1,\,0,\,1\}$$\end{document}{-1,0,1}, whereas in the triangular lattice in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$d=2$$\end{document}d=2 angles are multiples of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\pi /3$$\end{document}π/3, and their cosines belong to \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\{-1,\,-1/2,\,0,\,1/2,\,1\}$$\end{document}{-1,-1/2,0,1/2,1}.

As stated in Section 4, any bare \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\tau $$\end{document}τ induces a permutation \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\sigma \in S_{{{\,\textrm{cycl}\,}}}(V)$$\end{document}σ∈Scycl(V) on vertices. See Figure 6 for an example of the reflected permutation \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\rho $$\end{document}ρ in the square lattice, and Figure 7 for the triangular lattice. We can then see that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$K(\mathcal {E})=K(\mathcal {E}')$$\end{document}K(E)=K(E′) and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${{\,\textrm{sign}\,}}(\tau )={{\,\textrm{sign}\,}}(\rho )$$\end{document}sign(τ)=sign(ρ). Furthermore, with simple calculations of inner products we have 5.8\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} & \overline{M}\left( \eta ^\alpha (v),\,\eta (\sigma (v))\right) + \overline{M}\left( R_{v,\eta }(\eta ^\alpha (v)),\,\eta (\sigma (v))\right) \nonumber \\ & \quad = 2\cos \left( \frac{2\pi \alpha (v)}{p}\right) \,\overline{M}(\eta (v),\,\eta (\sigma (v))). \end{aligned}$$\end{document}M¯ηα(v),η(σ(v))+M¯Rv,η(ηα(v)),η(σ(v))=2cos2πα(v)pM¯(η(v),η(σ(v))). Observe that these cancellations happen in the hypercubic, triangular and hexagonal lattices due to their high symmetries.

With 5.8 in mind, Equation (5.7) becomes 5.9\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} (-1)^{\sum _v k_v} \sum _{\mathcal {E}:\, |\mathcal {E}_v|\ge k_v \, \forall v} \sum _{\begin{array}{c} \eta :\,V\rightarrow E(V)\\ \eta (v)\in \mathcal {E}_v\; \forall v \end{array}}\;\sum _{\sigma \in S_{{{\,\textrm{cycl}\,}}}(V)} \sum _{\alpha :\,V\rightarrow \{0,\,\ldots ,\,p-1\}} \sum _{\tau \in S_{{{\,\textrm{bare}\,}}}(\mathcal {E};\,\eta ,\,\sigma ,\,\alpha )} {{\,\textrm{sign}\,}}(\tau ) \times \nonumber \\ \times \prod _{f\in \mathcal {E}\setminus \eta ^{\alpha }(V)} \overline{M}\left( f,\,\tau (f)\right) \prod _{v\in V}K(\mathcal {E}_v) \gamma _\alpha (v) \underbrace{\prod _{v\in V} \partial ^{(1)}_{\eta (v)}\partial ^{(2)}_{\eta (\sigma (v))} g_{U}\left( \eta (v),\,\eta (\sigma (v))\right) }_{(\star )}. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}(-1)∑vkv∑E:|Ev|≥kv∀v∑η:V→E(V)η(v)∈Ev∀v∑σ∈Scycl(V)∑α:V→{0,…,p-1}∑τ∈Sbare(E;η,σ,α)sign(τ)××∏f∈E\ηα(V)M¯f,τ(f)∏v∈VK(Ev)γα(v)∏v∈V∂η(v)(1)∂η(σ(v))(2)gUη(v),η(σ(v))⏟(⋆). The factor \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(\star )$$\end{document}(⋆), which accounts for the interactions between different points, only depends on the entry directions given by \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\eta $$\end{document}η, not on the exit directions \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\eta ^{\alpha }$$\end{document}ηα. This is the key cancellation to obtain expressions of the form (4.2), up to constant.

We rewrite expression (5.9) as 5.10\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \sum _{\begin{array}{c} \eta :\,V\rightarrow E(V)\\ \eta (v)\in E_{v}\; \forall v \end{array}}\; \sum _{\sigma \in S_{{{\,\textrm{cycl}\,}}}(V)} \prod _{v\in V} \partial ^{(1)}_{\eta (v)}\partial ^{(2)}_{\eta (\sigma (v))} g_{U}\left( \eta (v),\,\eta (\sigma (v))\right) \times \nonumber \\ \times \prod _{v\in V}\underbrace{(-1)^{k_v} \sum _{\begin{array}{c} \mathcal {E}_v:\, \mathcal {E}_v\ni \eta (v)\\ |\mathcal {E}_v|\ge k_v \end{array}} K(\mathcal {E}_v) \sum _{\alpha =0}^{p-1} \gamma _\alpha (v) \sum _{\tau \in S_{{{\,\textrm{bare}\,}}}(\mathcal {E};\,\eta ,\,\sigma ,\,\alpha )}{{\,\textrm{sign}\,}}(\tau ) \prod _{f\in \mathcal {E}_v\setminus \{\eta ^\alpha (v)\}}\overline{M}\left( f,\,\tau (f)\right) }_{(\star \star )}. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}∑η:V→E(V)η(v)∈Ev∀v∑σ∈Scycl(V)∏v∈V∂η(v)(1)∂η(σ(v))(2)gUη(v),η(σ(v))××∏v∈V(-1)kv∑Ev:Ev∋η(v)|Ev|≥kvK(Ev)∑α=0p-1γα(v)∑τ∈Sbare(E;η,σ,α)sign(τ)∏f∈Ev\{ηα(v)}M¯f,τ(f)⏟(⋆⋆). Remark that if \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\eta ^\alpha (v) \not \in {\mathcal {E}}$$\end{document}ηα(v)∉E, the set \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$S_{{{\,\textrm{bare}\,}}}(\mathcal {E};\,\eta ,\,\sigma ,\,\alpha )$$\end{document}Sbare(E;η,σ,α) is empty, and therefore not contributing to the sum.

Notice that all entries of the type \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\overline{M}(e,\,\tau (e))$$\end{document}M¯(e,τ(e)) in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(\star \star )$$\end{document}(⋆⋆) are discrete double gradients of the Green function of the full lattice \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textbf{L}$$\end{document}L (see Equation (5.6)). In the following we will prove that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(\star \star )$$\end{document}(⋆⋆) does not depend on the choice of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\eta $$\end{document}η nor \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\sigma $$\end{document}σ. The value of the term \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(\star \star )$$\end{document}(⋆⋆) will give the constants \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$C_\textbf{L}^{(k_v)}$$\end{document}CL(kv) (up to an overall minus sign).

Using \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\sigma $$\end{document}σ, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\eta $$\end{document}η and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\alpha $$\end{document}α, we have been able to isolate in (5.10) an expression that depends only on permutations of vertices. To complete the proof we will perform a “surgery” to better understand expression (5.10). This surgery aims at decoupling the local behavior of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\tau $$\end{document}τ at a vertex versus the jumps of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\tau $$\end{document}τ between different vertices.

Lemma 5.4

(Surgery of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\tau $$\end{document}τ) Fix \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$v\in V$$\end{document}v∈V and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {E}$$\end{document}E, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\eta $$\end{document}η, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\sigma $$\end{document}σ, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\alpha $$\end{document}α as above. Remark 11

We highlight once again that, with the technical exception of Step 1, all the other steps follow in much the same way for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textbf{H}$$\end{document}H, in which case \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$p=3$$\end{document}p=3, and the value of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$c_{\textbf{H}}$$\end{document}cH can also be calculated, obtaining \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$c_{\textbf{H}} = 3/2$$\end{document}cH=3/2.
